_source line 2124 · 2026-05-25T11:14:52.154046+00:00_

20	If the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\alpha\|\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\|_\infty < \gamma(p_t, \epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \geq t_H + 1$.
21	\end{observation}
22	
23	ECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\Delta\mathrm{ECE} \approx \Delta C > 0$ whenever $\Delta\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}), yielding a three-stage cascade $\{H, \mathrm{ECE}\} \to \mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$.
24	This ordering holds in 14/15 experiments at $\alpha \geq 0.30$, with entropy and ECE consistently reaching onset at generation~1 and distinct-$n$ following at generation~2 ($t_H{=}t_{\mathrm{ECE}}{=}1$, $t_D{=}2$; \S\ref{sec:regime}; Appendix~\ref{app:moment_cascade}).
25	The one exception---SmolLM3-3B (3/8 at $\alpha{=}1.0$; Table~\ref{tab:generalization})---shows entropy decoupling from diversity, violating the monotonic-decline assumption that underlies both Observations.
26	The cascade is also specific to diversity metrics: first-differencing eliminates canary$\to$perplexity associations entirely ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; \S\ref{sec:regime}), confirming that the temporal structure is channel-specific rather than global.
27	
28	\subsection{Design Considerations}
29	\label{sec:pitfalls}
30	
31	We use \emph{Granger precedence}---temporal predictive improvement, where past values of $X$ improve prediction of $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history---to assess whether canary metrics anticipate downstream degradation~\citep{granger1969investigating}.

1	\FloatBarrier
2	\section{Method}
3	\label{sec:method}
4	
5	\subsection{Cascade Ordering}
6	\label{sec:cascade}
7	
8	If distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.
9	We formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (notation in Appendix~\ref{app:background}; derivations in Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}).
10	
11	\begin{observation}[Entropy responds before diversity]\label{thm:entropy_leads}
12	Under mode amplification: \textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\epsilon$-effective support $S_\epsilon(p) {=} |\{i: p_i > \epsilon\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.
13	\end{observation}
14	
15	Intuitively, entropy responds to any redistribution of probability mass (a continuous change), whereas diversity metrics like distinct-$n$ only change when tokens are pushed entirely below the generation threshold (a discrete event).
16	This measurement asymmetry ensures entropy shifts before diversity under gradual mode amplification.
17	The margin $\gamma = \min_{i: p_i > \epsilon}(p_i - \epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.
18	
19	\begin{observation}[Diversity lag $\geq 1$ generation]\label{prop:div_lag}
20	If the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\alpha\|\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\|_\infty < \gamma(p_t, \epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \geq t_H + 1$.
21	\end{observation}
22	
23	ECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\Delta\mathrm{ECE} \approx \Delta C > 0$ whenever $\Delta\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}), yielding a three-stage cascade $\{H, \mathrm{ECE}\} \to \mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$.
24	This ordering holds in 14/15 experiments at $\alpha \geq 0.30$, with entropy an

1220	(1)~It confirms that the canary$\to$diversity temporal precedence is not driven by a shared trend (generation number): removing the linear trend \emph{strengthens} rather than eliminates the lagged association.
1221	(2)~It shows that the temporal signal grows with contamination level: at $\alpha{=}0.00$, lagged partial $|r|{=}0.524$ (marginal significance); at $\alpha{=}0.75$, lagged partial $|r|{=}0.913$ (universal significance, 18/18).
1222	This monotonic growth parallels the MI analysis and confirms that the blind-zone boundary reflects statistical power rather than a qualitative transition in the coupling structure.
1223	
1224	\paragraph{Null behavior.}
1225	At $\alpha{=}0.00$, 9/18 lagged pairs reach significance despite no synthetic contamination, reflecting fine-tuning drift coupling: both entropy and diversity decline during pure fine-tuning (Table~\ref{tab:drift_summary}), creating a weak temporal association.
1226	These null-condition correlations are substantially weaker than treatment-condition correlations ($0.524$ vs.\ $0.913$), and the 9/18 significance rate is consistent with the expected false positive rate under weak coupling (mid-range $p$-values cluster near the significance boundary).
1227	
1228	
1229	\section{Moment Cascade Analysis}

1	\FloatBarrier
2	\section{Method}
3	\label{sec:method}
4	
5	\subsection{Cascade Ordering}
6	\label{sec:cascade}
7	
8	If distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.
9	We formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (notation in Appendix~\ref{app:background}; derivations in Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}).
10	
11	\begin{observation}[Entropy responds before diversity]\label{thm:entropy_leads}
12	Under mode amplification: \textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\epsilon$-effective support $S_\epsilon(p) {=} |\{i: p_i > \epsilon\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.
13	\end{observation}
14	
15	Intuitively, entropy responds to any redistribution of probability mass (a continuous change), whereas diversity metrics like distinct-$n$ only change when tokens are pushed entirely below the generation threshold (a discrete event).
16	This measurement asymmetry ensures entropy shifts before diversity under gradual mode amplification.
17	The margin $\gamma = \min_{i: p_i > \epsilon}(p_i - \epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.
18	
19	\begin{observation}[Diversity lag $\geq 1$ generation]\label{prop:div_lag}
20	If the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\alpha\|\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\|_\infty < \gamma(p_t, \epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \geq t_H + 1$.
21	\end{observation}
22	
23	ECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\Delta\mathrm{ECE} \approx \Delta C > 0$ whenever $\Delta\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}), yielding a three-stage cascade $\{H, \mathrm{ECE}\} \to \mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$.
24	This ordering holds in 14/15 experiments at $\alpha \geq 0.30$, with entropy an

\centering
\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_window_sensitivity.pdf}
\caption{Detection stability as a function of minimum observation window $T_{\min}$ (Pythia-410M, $\alpha{=}1.0$, 4~seeds). PermG forward detection (6/8) and consensus classification ($K{\geq}4$) both stabilize at $T_{\min}{\geq}5$, with no significance flips for $T_{\min} \in [5, 10]$. This robustness confirms that the choice of $T_{\min}{=}5$ in the main analysis does not drive results.}
\label{fig:window_sensitivity}
\end{figure}

21:\begin{figure}[t]

30:Sigmoid regression yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}); cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\to}6/8$) and specification-invariance across 1{,}160 configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:spec_curve}) argue for a genuine transition (Fisher exact: $p = 3.45 \times 10^{-11}$).
58:The PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; Appendix~\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\emph{blind} ($\alpha \leq 0.45$, 0/8), \emph{onset} ($\alpha \approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \emph{detection} ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\alpha \approx 0.60$ (0/8).
75:\begin{figure}[t]
77:\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
80:\end{figure}
86:The phase diagram (Figure~\ref{fig:phase_diagram}) characterizes detectability on Pythia-410M + WikiText-103; this section tests whether the detection signal generalizes across architectures at the most favorable contamination level ($\alpha{=}1.0$).
88:Forward detection reaches ${\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_arch_heatmap}).
98:\begin{figure}[t]
100:\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf}
103:\end{figure}
111:Predictive cessation caps peak perplexity at $56.99$ vs.\ $157.09$ reactive, a 64\% reduction (Figure~\ref{fig:intervention}); the result replicates on Pythia-1.4B with comparable effect size (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_discussion}).

4	When language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.
5	This process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.
6	As machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.
7	
8	This threat motivates a fundamental question: \emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}
9	The answer is less clear than it might appear.
10	Iterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\citep{scheffer2009early} (\S\ref{sec:related_work}).
11	More fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.
12	Detection is effective only above $\alpha_c \approx 0.50$; substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\sim}40\%$ distinct-1 decline) occurs undetected below this threshold, making the framework a confirmation tool for ongoing collapse rather than an early-warning system.
13	
14	\begin{figure}[t]
15	\centering
16	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_phase_diagram.pdf}
17	\caption{Phase diagram (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG). FDR-significant canary$\to$diversity pairs across $\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fi

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M~\citep{biderman2023pythia} as the primary architecture.
8	Training data is drawn from WikiText-103~\citep{merity2017pointer} following the replace paradigm of~\citet{shumailov2024curse}: each generation fine-tunes on a mixture of $\alpha$ synthetic and $(1{-}\alpha)$ real data, generates 5{,}000 replacement samples, and fully replaces the synthetic pool (hyperparameters in Appendix~\ref{app:training_details}).
9	We run $T{=}10$ generations across 13 contamination levels ($\alpha \in \{0.00, \ldots, 1.00\}$) with multi-seed replication, yielding ${\sim}1{,}500$ statistical tests after BH-FDR correction~\citep{benjamini1995controlling}.
10	We track two \emph{canary metrics} (token entropy and ECE) and three \emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1, distinct-2~\citep{li2016diversity}, and perplexity (distinct-3: Appendix~\ref{app:distinct3}).
11	PermG uses 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series~\citep{shojaie2022granger} with maximum lag~5 (power analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}).
12	
13	
14	\subsection{Steep Detection Onset}
15	\label{sec:regime}
16	
17	Diversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\%$ even at $\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\pm}0.3\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.25$.
18	Despite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger detectability is strongly regime-dependent.
19	PermG finds 0/8 canary$\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (Clopper--Pearson 95\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) at $\alpha{=}0.50$, uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.
20

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Training Details and Metric Drift}
4	\label{app:training_details}
5	\label{app:drift}
6	
7	\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}
8	Each generation fine-tunes for 5{,}000 steps using AdamW (learning rate $5 \times 10^{-5}$, 500 warmup steps, weight decay $0.01$, batch size 4 for Pythia-410M, 32 for GPT-2-medium).
9	Generation uses temperature $1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512 tokens, producing 5{,}000 samples per generation from 5{,}000-sample subsets of WikiText-103.
10	Canary metrics: token entropy $H(p_\theta | \text{context})$ over 1{,}000 held-out samples; ECE with 15 bins.
11	Additional downstream metrics (distinct-3, effective rank, Self-BLEU) appear in subsequent appendix sections.
12	
13	\paragraph{Architecture-specific training.}
14	Table~\ref{tab:arch_hyperparams} reports training hyperparameters for each architecture in the cross-architecture evaluation (\S\ref{sec:generalization}).
15	All models use the same generation parameters (temperature~$1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512) to control for decoding-strategy confounds.
16	Batch sizes are adjusted for GPU memory constraints; learning rates follow the respective model families' recommended fine-tuning ranges.

903	\label{app:fpr_independence}

996	The concentration of false positives in token-entropy$\to$distinct pairs at seeds~45 and 50 suggests stochastic null-condition coupling in these specific initializations rather than a systematic calibration failure.
997	$K{\geq}3$ consensus FPR on the 6-pair basis is 6/36${=}$16.7\%; of these 6 false positives, only 2 include PermG (both at s50)---the remaining 4 are driven by high-FPR methods (NaiveXCorr $+$ Threshold $+$ TY) reaching consensus without PermG participation.
998	This decomposition clarifies why $K{\geq}3$ FPR exceeds standalone PermG FPR: the consensus mechanism aggregates false positives from multiple methods rather than filtering them.
999	
1000	\FloatBarrier
1001	\section{Sigmoid Transition Shape}
1002	\label{app:sigmoid_shape}
1003	
1004	Sigmoid regression on the original 13-point $T{=}10$ grid yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ but $k = 478 \pm 475$ ($\mathrm{SE}(k) \approx k$), indicating that the coarse grid cannot constrain the transition shape.
1005	Dense $\alpha$ sampling (17~values at $T{=}10$) resolves this ambiguity: formal likelihood-ratio testing strongly favors a smooth sigmoid over a literal step function ($\Delta\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), with fitted steepness $k{=}15.7$ (95\% CI $[9.4, 28.2]$).
1006	The transition is steep but finite-width, not a literal discontinuity.
1007	This observed steepness exceeds what smooth signal models predict: VAR(1) simulations under H0 (linear, $k{=}6.1$) and H0$'$ (concave, $k{=}4.9$) produce onset sigmoids with $k{=}4.9$--$6.1$ (\S\ref{app:power_sim}), yielding steepness ratios $k_{\mathrm{obs}} / k_{\mathrm{H0}} \approx 2.6$--$3.2$ (CI lower bound ratio ${\approx}2$).
1008	
1009	\paragraph{Combined $T{=}10 + T{=}20$ fit.}
1010	Fitting a single sigmoid to both $T$ values yields $k{=}279.3$ (SE${=}527.6$), $\alpha_c{=}0.451$, $y_{\max}{=}0.75$, AIC${=}79.86$.

12	
13	# Model Collapse — Writing Skill (8 deep-read papers)
14	
15	## 叙事原型
16	
17	**主流: "破局型"** (5/8) — "先前定性 X 有误/不够，我们发现机制 Z"。He2025: "方差太慢→熵"; Dohmatob2025: "mixing 应管用→不管用"; Noci2024: "已知 depth collapse→发现 width collapse"。**次主流: "统一框架型"** (2/8) — 解释矛盾结论属不同 regime。Kazdan2025, Dohmatob2024。高。
18	
19	## Hook 策略
20	
21	**"数据危机" + 具体数字** (5/8): "17T tokens" (Guo), "ChatGPT=0.1% daily global words" (Dohmatob2024), "LAION-5B 已含合成图" (Alemohammad)。高。
22	
23	**命名现象**: "MAD cow disease" (Alemohammad) 是领域最高频引用 hook。命名让概念可传播: "MAD", "Strong Model Collapse", "generalization-to-memorization transition"。**4/8**。中。
24	
25	**直接挑战范式**: "Within the paradigm underpinning ChatGPT and Llama..." (Dohmatob2025)——对标 scaling law 提升 stakes。**2/8**。中。
26	
27	## Intro 结构
28

1	\begin{abstract}
2	When language models train iteratively on their own output, the resulting distributional collapse is difficult to detect: the time series are short, the signal grows sub-linearly with contamination, and---as we demonstrate---the choice of statistical method alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null.
3	We identify a steep detection onset in iterative model collapse: Granger-based testing finds zero significant canary$\to$downstream pairs through 45\% synthetic contamination, then jumps to reliable detection (6/8 pairs; 95\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) within a two-percentage-point interval at $\alpha_c \approx 0.50$, defining three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection).
4	This transition emerges from a five-method comparison where null-control false-positive rates span 7\% to 94\%; permutation Granger causality, the lowest-FPR method (6.7\%, CI $[1.8\%, 16.2\%]$), serves as the primary detector.
5	Cross-architecture evaluation at full synthetic replacement ($\alpha{=}1.0$) across five model families (345M--6.9B) supports forward detection in 11/15 conditions.
6	Token entropy temporally precedes diversity collapse by at least one generation, enabling a proof-of-concept intervention that reduces peak perplexity by 64\% ($\alpha{=}1.0$)\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/distrib_canary_collapse-3463/}.}.
7	\end{abstract}
8

616	\section{Cross-Seed Consistency at $\alpha{=}0.60$}
617	\label{app:alpha060}
618	
619	The non-monotonic detection gap at $\alpha{=}0.60$ (\S\ref{sec:dose_response}) is consistent across seeds: PermG yields 0/8 FDR-significant forward pairs for both seed~42 (v4 recompilation) and seed~43.
620	An earlier pipeline version (v3, seed~42) reported 3/8; the discrepancy is resolved by the d120 recompilation that corrected FPR calibration.
621	
622	\paragraph{Detailed metrics at $\alpha{=}0.60$.}
623	Despite PermG non-detection, metric drift at $\alpha{=}0.60$ is substantial: token entropy $-28.5\%$ (gen-0: $3.234$, gen-10: $2.311$), ECE $+1561\%$ ($0.0108 \to 0.179$), perplexity $+92.3\%$ ($26.95 \to 51.83$).
624	Raw (pre-FDR) $p$-values are marginal: ECE$\to$distinct-1 $p{=}0.034$, ECE$\to$distinct-2 $p{=}0.047$---both would pass at uncorrected $\alpha{=}0.05$ but fail BH-FDR correction ($q{=}0.05$, 8 simultaneous tests).
625	This indicates that the signal exists but falls below the corrected significance threshold, consistent with the temporal-compression interpretation.
626	
627	\paragraph{Cross-$T$ behavior.}
628	The sync-dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$ persists across observation windows: $T{=}5$: 0/8; $T{=}10$: 0/8 (both s42, s43); $T{=}15$: 0/8 (s42); $T{=}20$: 0/8 (s42), 3/8 (s43, partial dissolution).
629	Full dissolution occurs only at $T{=}25$: PermG 5/8 (s42), $K{\geq}3$ consensus 6/8.
630	The pattern---persistent non-detection for $T{\leq}20$, resolution at $T{=}25$---is consistent with $\sqrt{T}$ power accumulation eventually resolving a compressed (but non-zero) temporal lag.
631	
632	\paragraph{Scale dependence.}
633	Pythia-1.4B does \emph{not} exhibit the sync-dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$: PermG detects 4/8 (s42) and 4/6 (s43) at $T{=}10$, rising to 6/8 and 6/6 at $T{=}15$.

_source line 2634 · 2026-05-25T11:39:47.621764+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.
5	This process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.
6	As machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.
7	
8	This threat motivates a fundamental question: \emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}
9	The answer is less clear than it might appear.
10	Iterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\citep{scheffer2009early} (\S\ref{sec:related_work}).
11	More fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.
12	Detection is effective only above $\alpha_c \approx 0.50$; substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\sim}40\%$ distinct-1 decline) occurs undetected below this threshold, making the framework a confirmation tool for ongoing collapse rather than an early-warning system.
13	
14	\begin{figure}[H]
15	\centering
16	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_phase_diagram.pdf}
17	\caption{Phase diagram (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG). FDR-significant canary$\to$diversity pairs across $\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fi

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
  "old_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nWhen language models train iteratively on their own output, the resulting distributional collapse is difficult to detect: the time series are short, the signal grows sub-linearly with contamination, and---as we demonstrate---the choice of statistical method alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null.\nWe identify a steep detection onset in iterative model collapse: Granger-based testing finds zero significant canary$\\to$downstream pairs through 45\\% synthetic contamination, then jumps to reliable detection (6/8 pairs; 95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) within a two-percentage-point interval at $\\alpha_c \\approx 0.50$, defining three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection).\nThis transition emerges from a five-method comparison where null-control false-positive rates span 7\\% to 94\\%; permutation Granger causality, the lowest-FPR method (6.7\\%, CI $[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$), serves as the primary detector.\nCross-architecture evaluation at full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) across five model families (345M--6.9B) supports forward detection in 11/15 conditions.\nToken entropy temporally precedes diversity collapse by at least one generation, enabling a proof-of-concept intervention that reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% ($\\alpha{=}1.0$)\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/distrib_canary_collapse-3463/}.}.\n\\end{abstract}",
  "new_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nAs machine-generated text proliferates online, language models increasingly risk training on their own output---a feedback loop that drives iterative model collapse, progressively eroding lexical diversity and calibration across generations.\nWhile collapse dynamics have been studied theoretically and empirically, \\emph{detecting} its onset remains an open challenge: iterative training produces short, noisy time series ($T{<}15$) that resist standard early-warning diagnostics, model collapse unfolds as a first-order transition that precludes critical-slowing-down signals, and---crucially---no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem.\nWe address this gap by mapping the \\emph{detectability landscape} of iterative collapse---how detection outcomes vary across contamination levels, observation windows, and statistical methods---on five model families (345M--6.9B).\nA steep detection onset emerges: permutation Granger testing finds zero significant canary$\\to$downstream pairs through 45\\% synthetic contamination, then jumps to reliable detection (6/8 pairs; 95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) within two percentage points, defining blind, onset, and detection regimes.\nAcross five methods, null-control false-positive rates span 7\\%--94\\%, revealing that method choice alone determines whether collapse appears detectable.\nToken entropy temporally precedes diversity decline by ${\\geq}1$ generation, enabling proof-of-concept intervention that reduces peak perplexity by 64\\%\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/distrib_canary_collapse-3463/}.}.\n\\end{abstract}"
}
```

1	\FloatBarrier
2	\section{Method}
3	\label{sec:method}
4	
5	\subsection{Cascade Ordering}
6	\label{sec:cascade}
7	
8	If distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.
9	We formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (notation in Appendix~\ref{app:background}; derivations in Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}).
10	
11	\begin{observation}[Entropy responds before diversity]\label{thm:entropy_leads}
12	Under mode amplification: \textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\epsilon$-effective support $S_\epsilon(p) {=} |\{i: p_i > \epsilon\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.
13	\end{observation}
14	
15	Intuitively, entropy responds to any redistribution of probability mass (a continuous change), whereas diversity metrics like distinct-$n$ only change when tokens are pushed entirely below the generation threshold (a discrete event).
16	This measurement asymmetry ensures entropy shifts before diversity under gradual mode amplification.
17	The margin $\gamma = \min_{i: p_i > \epsilon}(p_i - \epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.
18	
19	\begin{observation}[Diversity lag $\geq 1$ generation]\label{prop:div_lag}
20	If the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\alpha\|\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\|_\infty < \gamma(p_t, \epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \geq t_H + 1$.
21	\end{observation}
22	
23	ECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\Delta\mathrm{ECE} \approx \Delta C > 0$ whenever $\Delta\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}), yielding a three-stage cascade $\{H, \mathrm{ECE}\} \to \mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$.
24	This ordering holds in 14/15 experiments at $\alpha \geq 0.30$, with entropy an
…[truncated 7320 chars]

_source line 2766 · 2026-05-25T12:10:37.961482+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	Our Granger-precedence claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\citep{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}: bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and the FPR CI ($[1.8\%, 16.2\%]$) means Type~I error may be non-negligible.
4	Four of fifteen cross-architecture conditions do not confirm forward detection (\S\ref{sec:generalization}), each isolating a distinct PermG assumption violation---precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B)---requiring architecture-specific null calibration before deployment (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_discussion}).
5	More broadly, our observation sequences ($T{=}10$--$25$) are far shorter than econometric standards ($T{\geq}100$); permutation testing avoids asymptotic assumptions but cannot recover power lost to short samples~\citep{shojaie2022granger}; AR(1) simulation confirms first-differencing controls size at these lengths (Appendix~\ref{app:nonstationarity}).
6	Bivariate tests are susceptible to omitted variable bias---first-differencing removes shared linear trends but nonlinear confounds remain possible---and PermG tests linear precedence only; transfer entropy (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer_entropy}) yields 0/192 significant pairs.
7	Partial correlation provides supporting evidence: after controlling for generation number, contemporaneous associations weaken ($\bar{|r|}$: $0.590{\to}0.381$) while lagged correlations strengthen ($0.759{\to}0.841$), consistent with genuine temporal precedence rather than shared-trend artifacts (Appendix~\ref{app:mi_analysis}).
8	
9	Despite these individual limitations, the five methods exhibit complementary strengths.
10	Shapley value decomposition over all $2^5{=}32$ coalitions (Appendix~\ref{app:shapley}) shows DiffXCorr achieving the best combined Shapley contribution ($-0.299$) by detecting bidirectional coupling that directional methods miss, while PermG ranks best amon
…[truncated 1437 chars]

_source line 2768 · 2026-05-25T12:10:37.962014+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Iterative training collapse exhibits a steep detection onset: PermG detection jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs (95\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) within 2~percentage points of $\alpha_c \approx 0.50$, organizing three regimes (blind, onset, detection) that characterize when statistical monitoring can and cannot succeed.
5	The detection boundary shifts leftward with longer observation ($\alpha_c(T) \propto T^{-\gamma}$), and systematic method comparison reveals that single-method analyses are unreliable: null-control FPR spans 6.7\% (PermG; 95\% CI $[1.8\%, 16.2\%]$, 10~seeds) to 94.4\%.
6	Token entropy exhibits robust one-generation-ahead precedence over diversity collapse (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse at $\alpha{=}1.0$; ${\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 cross-architecture conditions at $\alpha{=}1.0$).
7	The blind zone below $\alpha_c$ constrains practical deployment to settings with sufficient contamination; evaluation at ${>}$7B parameters remains open.
8

_source line 2770 · 2026-05-25T12:11:03.495318+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	As machine-generated text proliferates online, language models increasingly risk training on their own output---a feedback loop that drives iterative model collapse, progressively eroding lexical diversity and calibration across generations.
3	While collapse dynamics have been studied theoretically and empirically, \emph{detecting} its onset remains an open challenge: iterative training produces short, noisy time series ($T{<}15$) that resist standard early-warning diagnostics, model collapse unfolds as a first-order transition that precludes critical-slowing-down signals, and---crucially---no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem.
4	We address this gap by mapping the \emph{detectability landscape} of iterative collapse---how detection outcomes vary across contamination levels, observation windows, and statistical methods---on five model families (345M--6.9B).
5	A steep detection onset emerges: permutation Granger testing finds zero significant canary$\to$downstream pairs through 45\% synthetic contamination, then jumps to reliable detection (6/8 pairs; 95\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) within two percentage points, defining blind, onset, and detection regimes.
6	Across five methods, null-control false-positive rates span 7\%--94\%, revealing that method choice alone determines whether collapse appears detectable.
7	Token entropy temporally precedes diversity decline by ${\geq}1$ generation, enabling proof-of-concept intervention that reduces peak perplexity by 64\%\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/distrib_canary_collapse-3463/}.}.
8	\end{abstract}
9

_source line 2802 · 2026-05-25T12:18:30.498978+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.
5	This process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.
6	As machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.
7	
8	This threat motivates a fundamental question: \emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}
9	The answer is less clear than it might appear.
10	Iterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\citep{scheffer2009early} (\S\ref{sec:related_work}).
11	More fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.
12	Detection is effective only above $\alpha_c \approx 0.50$; substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\sim}40\%$ distinct-1 decline) occurs undetected below this threshold, making the framework a confirmation tool for ongoing collapse rather than an early-warning system.
13	
14	\begin{figure}[H]
15	\centering
16	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_phase_diagram.pdf}
17	\caption{Phase diagram (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG). FDR-significant canary$\to$diversity pairs across $\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fi
…[truncated 3118 chars]

_source line 2806 · 2026-05-25T12:18:46.488977+00:00_

55	\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}
56	\label{sec:dose_response}
57	
58	The PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; Appendix~\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\emph{blind} ($\alpha \leq 0.45$, 0/8), \emph{onset} ($\alpha \approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \emph{detection} ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\alpha \approx 0.60$ (0/8).
59	The blind zone exhibits zero detection across all seeds and methods below $\alpha{=}0.45$, despite substantial diversity loss---a regime where collapse proceeds silently (a perplexity-threshold baseline also fails; Appendix~\ref{app:ppl_threshold}).
60	Onset is remarkably steep: the sigmoid Hill coefficient $k{=}15.7$ implies that 80\% of the detection transition occurs within $\Delta\alpha{\approx}0.05$ (transition width analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:sigmoid_shape}), approximately $3$--$4{\times}$ steeper than smooth-signal predictions (Appendix~\ref{app:power_sim}).
61	Above onset, the detection zone shows cross-seed consistency: all 3~fp32 seeds independently reach 6/8 at $\alpha{=}0.50$ and $\alpha{\geq}0.75$.
62	
63	At $\alpha{\approx}0.60$, all direction-checked methods fail (PermG 0/8, threshold onset 0/8, Toda--Yamamoto 1/8), while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection (6/8).
64	This pattern suggests that at intermediate contamination, entropy and diversity respond near-simultaneously, compressing the temporal lag below PermG's resolution at $T{=}10$.
65	The anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\sqrt{T}$ power scaling restoring directional separation as additional observations accumulate (Appendix~\ref{app:t20_comparison}).
66	
67	\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}
68	\label{sec:t20}
69	
70	Longer observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\alpha_c(5){\geq}1.0$, $\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling $\alpha_c(T) \propto T^{-\gamma}$ (\S\ref{sec:theory}).
71	Profile likelihood yield

  "old_string": "\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\nLonger observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling $\\alpha_c(T) \\propto T^{-\\gamma}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nProfile likelihood yields $\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$ (95\\% CI), confirming that detection improves with longer observation regardless of the specific exponent.\nThe mechanism is statistical power accumulation: each additional generation provides one more data point for the permutation test, and the signal-to-noise ratio grows as $\\sqrt{T}$, progressively resolving weaker contamination signals.\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this prediction directly: undetectable at $T{\\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains stable at $T{=}20$ (5/8) and $T{=}25$ (6/8), precisely traversing the blind$\\to$detection boundary as the theory predicts.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: PermG detection rate with sigmoid fit ($\\alpha_c{=}0.483$; shaded 95\\% CI). Regime bands mark blind, onset, sync-dip, and detection zones.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}",

Regime bands mark blind, onset, sync-dip, and detection zones.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nLonger observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling $\\alpha_c(T) \\propto T^{-\\gamma}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nProfile likelihood yields $\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$ (95\\% CI), confirming that detection improves with longer observation regardless of the specific exponent.\nThe mechanism is statistical power accumulation: each additional generation provides one more data point for the permutation test, and the signal-to-noise ratio grows as $\\sqrt{T}$, progressively resolving weaker contamination signals.\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this prediction directly: undetectable at $T{\\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains stable at $T{=}20$ (5/8) and $T{=}25$ (6/8), precisely traversing the blind$\\to$detection boundary as the theory predicts."

795	The practical consequence: at $T{=}10$, the differencing-induced power loss is the primary bottleneck for detecting weak contamination ($\alpha < 0.50$).
796	Extending observation to $T{=}20$ more than compensates: differenced power at $T{=}20$ exceeds raw power at $T{=}10$ for $\varphi \leq 0.95$, providing both valid size control and superior detection sensitivity.
797	This explains the large detection gains observed in the $T{=}20$ extension (\S\ref{app:t20_comparison}).

995	$K{\geq}3$ consensus FPR on the 6-pair basis is 6/36${=}$16.7\%; of these 6 false positives, only 2 include PermG (both at s50)---the remaining 4 are driven by high-FPR methods (NaiveXCorr $+$ Threshold $+$ TY) reaching consensus without PermG participation.
996	This decomposition clarifies why $K{\geq}3$ FPR exceeds standalone PermG FPR: the consensus mechanism aggregates false positives from multiple methods rather than filtering them.
997	
998	
999	\section{Sigmoid Transition Shape}
1000	\label{app:sigmoid_shape}
1001	
1002	Sigmoid regression on the original 13-point $T{=}10$ grid yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ but $k = 478 \pm 475$ ($\mathrm{SE}(k) \approx k$), indicating that the coarse grid cannot constrain the transition shape.
1003	Dense $\alpha$ sampling (17~values at $T{=}10$) resolves this ambiguity: formal likelihood-ratio testing strongly favors a smooth sigmoid over a literal step function ($\Delta\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), with fitted steepness $k{=}15.7$ (95\% CI $[9.4, 28.2]$).
1004	The transition is steep but finite-width, not a literal discontinuity.
1005	This observed steepness exceeds what smooth signal models predict: VAR(1) simulations under H0 (linear, $k{=}6.1$) and H0$'$ (concave, $k{=}4.9$) produce onset sigmoids with $k{=}4.9$--$6.1$ (\S\ref{app:power_sim}), yielding steepness ratios $k_{\mathrm{obs}} / k_{\mathrm{H0}} \approx 2.6$--$3.2$ (CI lower bound ratio ${\approx}2$).
1006	
1007	\paragraph{Combined $T{=}10 + T{=}20$ fit.}
1008	Fitting a single sigmoid to both $T$ values yields $k{=}279.3$ (SE${=}527.6$), $\alpha_c{=}0.451$, $y_{\max}{=}0.75$, AIC${=}79.86$.
1009	The 90\% transition width narrows from $[0.478, 0.487]$ (width$=0.0092$) at $T{=}10$ alone to $[0.443, 0.459]$ (width$=0.0158$) in the combined fit---a leftward shift of $\alpha_c$ by $0.031$ reflecting the $T{=}20$ data's detection at $\alpha{=}0.45$.

1143	\caption{Channel effect size comparison: Granger $F$-statistic for canary$\to$diversity versus canary$\to$perplexity channels (Pythia-410M, $\alpha{=}1.0$, 4~seeds). Diversity channels produce $20$--$28{\times}$ higher $F$-statistics than perplexity channels, reflecting the temporal structure difference: diversity correlations peak at canary-leading lags (81\% at lag${<}0$), while perplexity correlations are contemporaneous (50\% at lag${=}0$).}

1148	\section{Mutual Information and Partial Correlation}
1149	\label{app:mi_analysis}
1150	
1151	Gaussian MI lower bound ($\hat{I}_G = -\frac{1}{2}\log_2(1{-}r^2)$) between token entropy and distinct-$n$ remains positive throughout the blind zone (0.010--0.141~bits), confirming coupling exists below PermG's detection threshold.
1152	No discontinuity at $\alpha_c$: detection onset reflects power accumulation, not an information transition.
1153	
1154	\paragraph{MI across the blind zone.}
1155	Table~\ref{tab:mi_blind} reports Gaussian MI between token entropy and distinct-1 at each contamination level below $\alpha_c$.
1156	MI increases monotonically with $\alpha$, confirming that the entropy--diversity coupling strengthens continuously across the blind$\to$detection boundary.
1157	The detection onset at $\alpha_c$ therefore reflects a power threshold (sufficient signal-to-noise for PermG to detect the coupling) rather than a qualitative change in the underlying statistical relationship.

  "old_string": "\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe dense-sampling transition width ($\\Delta\\alpha{=}0.028$, corresponding to $\\alpha \\in [0.467, 0.495]$) is ${\\sim}3{\\times}$ narrower than smooth-model predictions (0.072--0.090), confirming the anomalous sharpness.\nIn practical terms, a difference of $\\Delta\\alpha{=}0.03$ in contamination fraction determines whether detection is impossible (0\\%) or reliable (75\\%)---this represents approximately 150 additional synthetic samples out of 5{,}000.\nThe narrow transition has implications for monitoring systems: slight increases in synthetic data fraction near the boundary produce large changes in detectability, creating a ``cliff effect'' where monitoring transitions from false security to reliable alarm with minimal change in the underlying contamination level.\nA system operating at $\\alpha{=}0.47$ (undetectable) and drifting to $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (detectable) would experience an abrupt shift from zero alarm rate to 75\\% alarm rate---a three percentage-point contamination increase produces a qualitative change in monitoring behavior.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/differencing_dmax.pdf}\n\\caption{First-differencing order selection diagnostic. The optimal differencing order $d_{\\max}$ is evaluated via ADF test statistics across all canary$\\to$downstream series at $\\alpha{=}1.0$. First-order differencing ($d{=}1$) achieves stationarity in all series (ADF $p{<}0.05$), confirming that higher-order differencing is unnecessary and would incur additional power loss.}\n\\label{fig:differencing_dmax}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Intervention Threshold Sensitivity}",
  "new_string": "\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/differencing_dmax.pdf}\n\\caption{First-differencing order selection diagnostic. 1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.
5	This process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.
6	As machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.
7	
8	\begin{figure}[H]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_phase_diagram.pdf}
11	\caption{Phase diagram (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG). FDR-significant canary$\to$diversity pairs across $\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fitted boundary (Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c}); $\diamond$: non-monotonic anomaly; $\star$: dissolution at $T{=}25$.}
12	\label{fig:phase_diagram}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	This threat motivates a fundamental question: \emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}
16	The answer is less clear than it might appear.
17	Iterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\citep{scheffer2009early} (\S\ref{sec:related_work}).
18	More fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.
19	Detection is effective only above $\alpha_c \approx 0.50$; substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\sim}40\%$ distin

766	\paragraph{ADF test results.}
767	Augmented Dickey--Fuller tests on first-differenced series reject the unit root hypothesis at $p{<}0.05$ in all 40 series (5~metrics $\times$ 4~seeds $\times$ 2~conditions), confirming that first-order differencing ($d{=}1$) is sufficient and higher-order differencing ($d{\geq}2$) would unnecessarily reduce power (Figure~\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}).
768	
769	\paragraph{Why not KPSS?}
770	We use ADF (null: unit root) rather than KPSS (null: stationarity) because ADF is more conservative for our purpose: rejecting the unit-root null provides positive evidence of stationarity, while KPSS's failure to reject would only indicate insufficient evidence against stationarity.
771	In the short-series regime ($T{\leq}25$), KPSS has limited power; ADF's rejection at $p{<}0.05$ across all differenced series provides stronger evidence.
772	
773	\paragraph{Power cost of first-differencing.}
774	First-differencing eliminates spurious regression but reduces effective power by discarding level information (Table~\ref{tab:diff_power_cost}).
775	Under the AR(1) DGP ($\varphi{=}0.50$, $\beta{=}0.6$), first-differenced PermG achieves 17.7\% power versus 36.8\% for raw PermG---a 52\% relative power reduction.
776	At $\varphi{=}0.95$ the cost narrows (28.7\% vs.\ 41.5\%, 31\% reduction) because the stronger persistence makes the raw test less rel

900	\section{PermG FPR Independence Analysis}
901	\label{app:fpr_independence}
902	
903	Table~\ref{tab:null_fpr_methods} reports null-control FPR across nine seeds (54 pairs, 6-pair basis); a tenth seed (s51) yields 0~PermG FP, giving pooled 4/60${=}$6.7\% (CI $[1.8\%, 16.2\%]$).
904	All four FP occur in te$\to$distinct pairs in seeds~45/50 (8/10 seeds yield 0~FP).

706	\section{Non-Stationarity Robustness Analysis}
707	\label{app:nonstationarity}
708	
709	Monte Carlo simulation (AR(1) DGP, $T{=}11$, 1{,}000 reps) shows PermG FPR inflates to ${\sim}10\%$ under strong non-stationarity ($\varphi{\geq}0.95$), while first-differencing maintains proper size ($4.2$--$6.6\%$) at the cost of reduced power (Table~\ref{tab:nonstationarity_sim}).
710	Our pipeline applies first-differencing before permutation testing, combining both advantages.
711	Under null conditions ($\alpha{=}0.0$), token entropy exhibits $\varphi{=}-0.51$ (far from the danger zone); first-differencing reduces distinct-1's high raw $\varphi$ ($0.81$--$0.97$, reflecting deterministic trend) to $-0.14$--$0.44$.
712	Our empirical FPR of 4/60 (6.7\%) confirms adequate size control.
713	
714	\begin{figure}[H]
715	\centering
716	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_ar1_diagnostic.pdf}
717	\caption{AR(1) coefficient estimates across five contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42). (a)~Token entropy: raw $\varphi$ transitions from negative ($-0.51$ at $\alpha{=}0.00$) to near-unit-root ($1.00$ at $\alpha{=}1.00$), but first-differencing keeps all values below 0.98. (b)~Distinct-1: raw $\varphi$ is uniformly high ($0.75$--$0.95$) even at null, driven by deterministic trend; first-differencing reduces all to ${\leq}0.24$. Dashed red line: $\varphi{=}0.95$ danger zone.}
718	\label{fig:ar1_diagnostic}
719	\end{figure}

1079	\section{Channel Effect Size Analysis}
1080	\label{app:channel_effect_size}
1081	
1082	First-differencing retains comparable $|r|$ for both diversity (0.952) and perplexity (1.00) channels, ruling out selective power loss.
1083	The Granger $F$-statistic asymmetry ($20$--$28{\times}$ higher for diversity) reflects temporal structure: diversity channels peak at canary-leading lags (81\% at lag${<}0$, median $-1$), while perplexity peaks at lag${=}0$ (contemporaneous co-movement).
1084	Granger tests correctly assign low $F$ to contemporaneous relationships.
1085	
1086	\paragraph{Lag structure decomposition.}
1087	Table~\ref{tab:lag_structure} reports the cross-correlation peak lag for each canary$\to$downstream channel, illustrating the structural difference that drives Granger detectability.
1088	
1089	\begin{table}[H]
1090	\centering
1091	\small
1092	\caption{Cross-correlation peak lag by channel (Pythia-410M, $\alpha{=}1.0$, 4~seeds). Negative lag = canary leads. Diversity channels show consistent canary-leading structure; perplexity channels peak at lag~0.}
1093	\label{tab:lag_structure}
1094	\begin{tabular}{lccl}
1095	\toprule
1096	Channel & Peak lag & $|r|$ at peak & Structure \\
1097	\midrule
1098	Entropy$\to$Dist-1 & $-1$ & 0.96 & canary leads \\
1099	Entropy$\to$Dist-2 & $-1$ & 0.95 & canary leads \\
1100	ECE$\to$Dist-1 & $-1$ & 0.94 & canary leads \\
1101	ECE$\to$Dist-2 & $-1$ & 0.93 & canary leads \\
1102	Entropy$\to$PPL & $0$ & 0.99 & contemporaneous \\
1103	ECE$\to$PPL & $0$ & 0.98 & contemporaneous \\
1104	\bottomrule
1105	\end{tabular}
1106	\end{table}
1107	
1108	The contemporaneous co-movement of canary$\to$perplexity explains why Granger tests fail ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$ after differencing): perplexity and entropy respond to the same underlying distributional shift at the same time step, so neither provides predictive improvement over the other's own history.
1109	This is not a limitation of the test but a correct inference---perplexity is not

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.
5	This process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.
6	As machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.
7	
8	This threat motivates a fundamental question: \emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}
9	The answer is less clear than it might appear.
10	Iterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\citep{scheffer2009early} (\S\ref{sec:related_work}).
11	More fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.
12	Detection is effective only above $\alpha_c \approx 0.50$; substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\sim}40\%$ distinct-1 decline) occurs undetected below this threshold, making the framework a confirmation tool for ongoing collapse rather than an early-warning system.
13	
14	We systematically map this \emph{detectability landscape}---how detection outcomes vary across contamination levels ($\alpha$), observation windows ($T$), and statistical methods---on Pythia-410M~\citep{biderman2023pythia} across 13 contamin
…[truncated 2717 chars]

  "old_string": "\\section{Non-Stationarity Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{app:nonstationarity}\n\nMonte Carlo simulation (AR(1) DGP, $T{=}11$, 1{,}000 reps) shows PermG FPR inflates to ${\\sim}10\\%$ under strong non-stationarity ($\\varphi{\\geq}0.95$), while first-differencing maintains proper size ($4.2$--$6.6\\%$) at the cost of reduced power (Table~\\ref{tab:nonstationarity_sim}).\nOur pipeline applies first-differencing before permutation testing, combining both advantages.\nUnder null conditions ($\\alpha{=}0.0$), token entropy exhibits $\\varphi{=}-0.51$ (far from the danger zone); first-differencing reduces distinct-1's high raw $\\varphi$ ($0.81$--$0.97$, reflecting deterministic trend) to $-0.14$--$0.44$.\nOur empirical FPR of 4/60 (6.7\\%) confirms adequate size control.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_ar1_diagnostic.pdf}\n\\caption{AR(1) coefficient estimates across five contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42). (a)~Token entropy: raw $\\varphi$ transitions from negative ($-0.51$ at $\\alpha{=}0.00$) to near-unit-root ($1.00$ at $\\alpha{=}1.00$), but first-differencing keeps all values below 0.98. (b)~Distinct-1: raw $\\varphi$ is uniformly high ($0.75$--$0.95$) even at null, driven by deterministic trend; first-differencing reduces all to ${\\leq}0.24$. Dashed red line: $\\varphi{=}0.95$ danger zone.}\n\\label{fig:ar1_diagnostic}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[H]",
  "new_string": "\\section{Non-Stationarity Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{app:nonstationarity}\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_ar1_diagnostic.pdf}\n\\caption{AR(1) coefficient estimates across five contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42). 946	Table~\ref{tab:per_seed_fpr_8pair} reports FPR on a per-seed, per-method basis for the original 3-seed cohort (s42, s43, s44; 8-pair basis including MAUVE), while Table~\ref{tab:per_seed_fpr_6pair} extends to the 6-seed expansion (s45--s51; 6-pair basis excluding MAUVE).

951	\caption{Bootstrap confidence intervals for FPR estimates (10{,}000 resamples, 10~seeds). PermG's 95\% CI ($[1.8\%, 16.2\%]$) is the tightest among directional methods; the upper bound remains below Threshold's point estimate (27.8\%). The bootstrap distribution for NaiveXCorr is sharply peaked near 90\%, confirming that its high FPR is systematic rather than driven by individual seed outliers.}
952	\label{fig:bootstrap_fpr}

1148	\label{app:mi_analysis}
1149	
1150	Gaussian MI lower bound ($\hat{I}_G = -\frac{1}{2}\log_2(1{-}r^2)$) between token entropy and distinct-$n$ remains positive throughout the blind zone (0.010--0.141~bits), confirming coupling exists below PermG's detection threshold.
1151	No discontinuity at $\alpha_c$: detection onset reflects power accumulation, not an information transition.
1152	
1153	\paragraph{MI across the blind zone.}
1154	Table~\ref{tab:mi_blind} reports Gaussian MI between token entropy and distinct-1 at each contamination level below $\alpha_c$.
1155	MI increases monotonically with $\alpha$, confirming that the entropy--diversity coupling strengthens continuously across the blind$\to$detection boundary.
1156	The detection onset at $\alpha_c$ therefore reflects a power threshold (sufficient signal-to-noise for PermG to detect the coupling) rather than a qualitative change in the underlying statistical relationship.

  "new_string": "\\begin{figure}[H]\\ContinuedFloat\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_shapley_contribution_bottom.pdf}\n\\caption[]{(\\textit{continued})}\n\\end{figure}\n\nShapley values~\\citep{shapley1953value} computed over all $2^5{=}32$ coalitions quantify each method's marginal contribution to FPR and FNR (Table~\\ref{tab:shapley_6pair}).\nDiffXCorr achieves the best combined Shapley ($-0.299$) but cannot separate forward from reverse; among directional methods, PermG ranks best ($-0.141$).\nThe 8-pair variant (including MAUVE) yields identical rankings.\n\n\\begin{table}[H]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Shapley value decomposition (6-pair, MAUVE excluded).   "old_string": "\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_partial_correlation_agg.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregated partial correlation analysis across contamination levels. Lagged canary$\\to$diversity correlations increase monotonically with $\\alpha$ (from $0.524$ at null to $0.913$ at $\\alpha{=}0.75$), while contemporaneous correlations plateau. The growing gap between lagged and contemporaneous partial $|r|$ with increasing contamination indicates that the temporal lead signal strengthens with signal-to-noise ratio.}\n\\label{fig:partial_corr_agg}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe partial correlation analysis serves two purposes:\n(1)~It confirms that the canary$\\to$diversity temporal precedence is not driven by a shared trend (generation number): removing the linear trend \\emph{strengthens} rather than eliminates the lagged association.\n(2)~It shows that the temporal signal grows with contamination level: at $\\alpha{=}0.00$, lagged partial $|r|{=}0.524$ (marginal significance); at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, lagged partial $|r|{=}0.913$ (universal significance, 18/18).\nThis monotonic growth parallels the MI analysis and confirms that the blind-zone boundary reflects statistical power rather than a qualitative transition in the coupling structure.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe partial correlation analysis serves two purposes:\n(1)~It confirms that the canary$\\to$diversity temporal precedence is not driven by a shared trend (generation number): removing the linear trend \\emph{strengthens} rather than eliminates the lagged association.\n(2)~It shows that the temporal signal grows with contamination level: at $\\alpha{=}0.00$, lagged partial $|r|{=}0.524$ (marginal significance); at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, lagged partial $|r|{=}0.913$ (universal significance, 18/18).\nThis monotonic growth parallels the MI analysis and confirms that the blind-zone boundary reflects statistical power rather than a qualitative transition in the coupling structure.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_partial_correlation_agg.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregated partial correlation analysis across contamination levels. Lagged canary$\\to$diversity correlations increase monotonically with $\\alpha$ (from $0.524$ at null to $0.913$ at $\\alpha{=}0.75$), while contemporaneous correlations plateau. The growing gap between lagged and contemporaneous partial $|r|$ with increasing contamination indicates that the temporal lead signal strengthens with signal-to-noise ratio.}\n\\label{fig:partial_corr_agg}\n\\end{figure}"
}
```
